In the limited asymmetric scheme the flux keeps the form of eq. 5 and its normal gradient, but the transverse gradient on the face combines the four transverse differences on the faces of the two adjacent cells, $\overline{\partial T/\partial y}|_{i+1/2,j} = L\{L[\partial T/\partial y|_{i,j-1/2}, \partial T/\partial y|_{i,j+1/2}], L[\partial T/\partial y|_{i+1,j-1/2}, \partial T/\partial y|_{i+1,j+1/2}]\}$ (eq. 17). The limiter $L$ is minmod, that of van Leer or the monotonised central (MC) limiter, $\mathrm{MC}(a, b) = \mathrm{minmod}[2\,\mathrm{minmod}(a, b), (a + b)/2]$ (eq. 18). Minmod returns the argument of smaller magnitude when both have the same sign, and zero otherwise. A limiter thus weights the interpolation towards the smaller argument when the two differ much, and returns zero when they differ in sign. The other quantities are interpolated as in the centred scheme (eqs. 6, 8).

In the symmetric scheme both gradients sit at the corners, so that both must be limited ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §4.2). The normal term on the face $(i + 1/2, j)$ is the mean of a north-biased flux at the corner above, $q^N_{xx,i+1/2,j+1/2} = -\overline{n\chi}\,\overline{b_x}^2 L_2(\partial T/\partial x|_{i+1/2,j}, \partial T/\partial x|_{i+1/2,j+1})$, and a south-biased flux at the corner below, with $j - 1$ in place of $j + 1$ (eqs. 19, 20). The limiter $L_2(a, b)$ returns the mean $(a + b)/2$, clipped to the interval between $\min(\alpha a, a/\alpha)$ and $\max(\alpha a, a/\alpha)$, with $0 < \alpha < 1$ (eq. 21). Unlike the standard limiters, it is not symmetric in its arguments. It reduces to the mean where the temperature is smooth, but keeps the interpolated gradient close to the gradient $a$ on the face itself and of its sign, so that the normal flux runs from higher to lower temperatures. The paper takes $\alpha = 3/4$ throughout. This interpolation can diffuse the chessboard pattern. The transverse gradient is limited as in eq. 17, so that for $\alpha = 1$ the scheme resembles the limited asymmetric one, apart from the interpolation of the field and of $n\chi$.

A third way limits by the entropy-like function $\dot s^* = -\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla T$, whose positivity everywhere guarantees that heat flows from higher to lower temperatures ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §5). The symmetric scheme has $\dot s^* \geq 0$ at the corners, but not necessarily on the faces. The entropy-limited symmetric scheme forms the normal terms with $L_2$, which makes their contribution to $\dot s^*$ non-negative, and evaluates $\dot s^* = -q_{x,i+1/2,j}\,\partial T/\partial x|_{i+1/2,j} - q_{y,i,j+1/2}\,\partial T/\partial y|_{i,j+1/2}$ at all four pairs of faces of a cell (eq. 22). Where it is negative, the transverse terms $q_{xy}$ and $q_{yx}$ are reduced until it vanishes. Where eq. 22 holds, the scheme reduces to the symmetric one, the least diffusive of all. It does not guarantee monotonicity, however. Where both gradients of a pair of faces vanish, eq. 22 holds for any fluxes, and the transverse terms can carry heat the wrong way. The overshoots are smaller than those of the centred schemes and are damped within a few steps, so that they are absent at late times (Figs. 4, 6, 7). Slope limiting of the transverse gradients at extrema, added to the entropy limiting, makes the scheme monotonic but more diffusive. For their simplicity and their mathematical properties the slope-limited schemes are preferred to this cumbersome method.

#### 2.3.5 Extrema and the entropy-like condition

For minmod, van Leer and MC, the combination $L[L(a, b), L(c, d)]$ is symmetric in its four arguments and vanishes unless all four share a sign ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §6.1). At a local extremum of temperature, the differences on opposite faces of the cell differ in sign, or one of them vanishes, so that the limited transverse gradients of eq. 17 vanish on all its faces. The fluxes there reduce to the normal terms, $-n\chi b_x^2\,\partial T/\partial x$ and $-n\chi b_y^2\,\partial T/\partial y$, which point down the gradient. A maximum of temperature thus cannot grow, nor a minimum fall, and the temperature stays positive.

For a density constant in time, multiplying eq. 1 by $T$ and integrating over space shows that $\int nT^2\,\mathrm{d}V$ changes at a rate proportional to $\int\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla T\,\mathrm{d}V = -\int n\chi|\nabla_\parallel T|^2\,\mathrm{d}V \leq 0$, if the surface terms vanish ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §6.2, eq. 23). The volume-averaged fluctuations of temperature thus cannot grow, and locally $\dot s^* \geq 0$. For the symmetric scheme, $\dot s^*$ at a corner equals $\overline{n\chi}(\overline{b_x}\,\overline{\partial T/\partial x} + \overline{b_y}\,\overline{\partial T/\partial y})^2 \geq 0$, and its sum gives the discrete counterpart of eq. 23 (eqs. 24, 25). Yet this condition at the corners does not keep the temperatures at the centres positive (2.3.3). For the asymmetric and the limited asymmetric schemes, an interpolation of the transverse terms by an arithmetic mean or by a limiter with $|L(a, b, c, d)| \leq (|a| + |b| + |c| + |d|)/4$ bounds the global function $-\int\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla T\,\mathrm{d}V/V$ from below by a sum of squares, so that it is non-negative (eqs. 26 to 37). Multiplying eq. 1 by $1/T$ instead gives the global condition on the entropy, $\dot S = \int n\chi|\nabla_\parallel T|^2/T^2\,\mathrm{d}V \geq 0$, for the entropy $S = nVk\ln(T^{1/(\gamma - 1)}/n)$ of an ideal gas, with a slightly modified interpolation (§6.2; App. A, eq. A.1). The local entropy function $\dot s = -\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla T/T^2$ differs from $\dot s^*$ by the factor $1/T^2$. A condition on the entropy that holds at some points does not make heat flow the right way locally, so that slope limiters remain necessary at extrema. **[GK]** As $e = nT/(\gamma - 1)$, the left side of eq. 23 lacks a factor 1/2, as $\partial_t\int nT^2\,\mathrm{d}V/[2(\gamma - 1)]$, which leaves the sign unchanged.

#### 2.3.6 Tests of the anisotropic schemes

The first test, from [Parrish and Stone](#parrish-stone-2005) (2005), diffuses a hot patch along circular field lines ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §7.1). On the Cartesian domain $[-1, 1] \times [-1, 1]$, the temperature is 12 for $0.5 < r < 0.7$ and $11\pi/12 < \theta < 13\pi/12$, and 10 elsewhere, with $r = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ and $\tan\theta = y/x$, so that it drops discontinuously at the edge of the patch (eq. 38). Fixed circular field lines are centred on the origin, the density is unity, the boundaries reflect the temperature, and the field and conduction vanish outside $r = 1$. The parallel coefficient is $\chi = 0.01$, without explicit perpendicular diffusion. The runs stop at $t = 200$, by which time the ring $0.5 < r < 0.7$ should have an almost uniform temperature, by conservation of energy 10.1667, while the gas outside it stays at 10. Grids of $50^2$, $100^2$, $200^2$ and $400^2$ cells are compared, by the errors in the norms $L_1$, $L_2$ and $L_\infty$ against this state, the extreme temperatures and the perpendicular numerical diffusion (Tables 1 to 4).

All schemes heat the annulus, and the limited ones diffuse more across the field ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §7.1, Tables 1 to 4, Fig. 6). In this test the perpendicular numerical diffusion grows with $\chi$ for all schemes. The centred schemes fall below the initial minimum even at late times, to 9.99 for the asymmetric and 9.95 for the symmetric scheme on the finest grid, and the symmetric scheme leaves a profile full of non-monotonic oscillations. The slope-limited schemes never fall below 10. The entropy-limited scheme undershoots early but returns to the minimum of 10 at late times, and with limiting at extrema it behaves like the slope-limited schemes. Minmod is much more diffusive than the limiters of van Leer and MC. On the finest grid, the ratio $\chi_{\perp,\mathrm{num}}/\chi_\parallel$ is $8.4 \times 10^{-6}$ for the symmetric scheme and $6.5 \times 10^{-5}$ for the symmetric scheme with MC. It is $5.9 \times 10^{-4}$ and $6.8 \times 10^{-4}$ for the asymmetric scheme without and with MC, $1.3 \times 10^{-3}$ for both schemes with the limiter of van Leer, $2.7 \times 10^{-3}$ for the entropy-limited scheme and $1.2 \times 10^{-2}$ with minmod. On the coarsest grid it runs from $2.8 \times 10^{-4}$ for the symmetric scheme through $7.2 \times 10^{-3}$ and $1.3 \times 10^{-2}$ for the symmetric and asymmetric schemes with MC to about $4.9 \times 10^{-2}$ with minmod. On the finest grid the $L_1$ error is 0.0032 for the symmetric scheme with MC and 0.0033 without it, 0.010 and 0.012 for the asymmetric scheme with and without MC, and 0.04 with minmod.

Strictly, a hot ring in a cooler background is not a steady state, as numerical perpendicular diffusion slowly spreads its heat until the whole box has one temperature ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §7.1). Eq. 1 gives a rough estimate of the time-averaged perpendicular numerical diffusion, $\langle\chi_{\perp,\mathrm{num}}\rangle = \int(T_\mathrm{f} - T_\mathrm{i})\,\mathrm{d}V/\int\mathrm{d}t\int\nabla^2T\,\mathrm{d}V$, with the integrals over the ring and $T_\mathrm{i}$ and $T_\mathrm{f}$ its initial and final temperatures (eq. 39, Fig. 8). The estimate agrees roughly with the measurement of the second test, and, as there, the symmetric scheme is the least diffusive. It converges with the spacing of the grid at the order 1.64 for the symmetric scheme with MC, 1.59 and 1.48 for the symmetric and asymmetric schemes with the limiter of van Leer, 1.18 and 1.07 for the symmetric and asymmetric schemes, and 1.14 for the asymmetric scheme with MC (Table 5). Minmod reaches 0.74, and the entropy-limited scheme only 0.22, or 0.28 with limiting at extrema.

A variant with the patch at 10 and the background at 0.1 follows the behaviour at very late times ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §7.1, Fig. 7). The minimum temperature of both centred schemes turns negative, even at late times, which coupled to the MHD equations would make the sound speed imaginary. The entropy-limited scheme undershoots slightly at early times and returns to the minimum of 0.1 after $t = 1$, and the slope-limited schemes keep it at 0.1 throughout.

The second test, from [Sovinec et al.](#sovinec-et-al-2004) (2004), measures the perpendicular numerical diffusion in a steady state ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §7.2). On the unit square $[-0.5, 0.5] \times [-0.5, 0.5]$, with the temperature held at zero on the boundaries and a density of unity, a source $Q = 2\pi^2\cos(\pi x)\cos(\pi y)$ added to eq. 1 drives the lowest eigenmode of the temperature (eq. 40). The field follows the flux function $\psi \propto \cos(\pi x)\cos(\pi y)$, whose lines are concentric about the origin, and the driven temperature is constant along them. The steady state $T = \chi_\perp^{-1}\cos(\pi x)\cos(\pi y)$ is then independent of $\chi_\parallel$, and with $\chi_\perp = 1$ the central value $T^{-1}(0, 0)$ measures the sum of the explicit and the numerical perpendicular diffusion. The paper takes $\chi_{\perp,\mathrm{num}} = |T^{-1}(0, 0) - T^{-1}_\mathrm{iso}(0, 0)|$, with $T_\mathrm{iso}$ the central temperature of the same discretisation in the isotropic limit $\chi_\parallel = \chi_\perp$. This is more accurate than the earlier convention $|T^{-1}(0, 0) - 1|$, which assumed that the discrete isotropic operator gives $T_\mathrm{iso}(0, 0) = 1$ exactly.

For $\chi_\parallel/\chi_\perp = 10$ and 100, the perpendicular numerical diffusion grows linearly with $\chi_\parallel$ for all schemes but the symmetric one ([Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) 2007, §7.2, Fig. 9). [Günter et al.](#gunter-et-al-2005) (2005) stress this property of the symmetric scheme for fusion, where $\chi_\parallel/\chi_\perp \sim 10^9$. At a reasonable resolution, the limited schemes are thus unsuited to anisotropies far above $10^4$, which rules out such applications, but only they behave physically at extrema. The error converges at roughly second order for all schemes except those with minmod, at about 0.9 (Table 6). Its order is 1.9 for both schemes with MC, 1.6 to 1.8 for the centred schemes and those with the limiter of van Leer, 1.95 for the entropy-limited scheme with limiting at extrema, and 2.4 and 3.0 for the entropy-limited scheme without it at the two anisotropies. The paper concludes that the slope-limited scheme with MC is the most accurate method that stays free of unphysical behaviour at large gradients of temperature (§8, Figs. 6, 8). The main advantage of the limited schemes is their robustness at such gradients, which suits dilute astrophysical plasmas, where a modest anisotropy may suffice. **[GK]** The public C version of Athena (3.1) forms its anisotropic flux in `HeatFlux_aniso` of `src/microphysics/conduction.c` by the limited asymmetric scheme, with the normal field on the face and the transverse field averaged over the centres of the two adjacent cells. Its `limiter4` nests `limiter2` as in eq. 17, and `limiter2` applies the limiter of van Leer, with the MC limiter left in a comment. The product of the density and the constant diffusivity is the arithmetic mean over the two cells, not the harmonic mean of eq. 8, and the temperature is taken as $P/\rho$. Athena++ declares the functions of anisotropic conduction but leaves them empty, as `athena-synthesis.md` records.

#### 2.3.7 Isotropic conduction calibrated against anisotropic conduction

Hydrodynamic simulations of clouds commonly make conduction isotropic and reduce it by an efficiency $f \ll 1$ that stands for the suppression by magnetic fields, a value that is largely unconstrained ([Kooij, Grønnow and Fraternali](#kooij-et-al-2021) 2021, [arXiv:2101.04684](https://arxiv.org/abs/2101.04684), §1). Isotropic conduction at the full Spitzer value evaporates clouds too fast compared with observations ([Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) 2016). Estimates of $f$ range from 0.2 for a tangled field ([Narayan and Medvedev](#narayan-medvedev-2001) 2001) to 0.001 to 0.01 ([Chandran and Cowley](#chandran-cowley-1998) 1998), and 0.1 is a typical choice (2.1.2; [Armillotta et al.](#armillotta-et-al-2017) 2017). A fast cloud, however, meets a field draped around it rather than a tangled one. Kooij et al. therefore compare hydrodynamic simulations in three dimensions with isotropic conduction at several values of $f$ against magnetohydrodynamic ones with anisotropic conduction, and find the $f$ for which isotropic conduction affects the cold gas as anisotropic conduction does (abstract; 2.8.8).

They modify the module of conduction of the code PLUTO ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2007) 2007, [2012](#mignone-et-al-2012)) so that only free electrons carry heat ([Kooij, Grønnow and Fraternali](#kooij-et-al-2021) 2021, §2.2). The flux becomes $\mathbf{q} = -f\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}\nabla T\,x_\mathrm{i}/(1 + \sigma)$, with $x_\mathrm{i}$ the ionisation fraction, which depends on temperature and is found as for the cooling (eq. 3). The Spitzer conductivity is $\kappa_\mathrm{Sp} = 1.84 \times 10^{-5}\,T_\mathrm{e}^{5/2}/\ln\Psi$ erg s$^{-1}$ K$^{-1}$ cm$^{-1}$, with the temperature of the electrons $T_\mathrm{e}$ equal to that of the gas and the Coulomb logarithm $\ln\Psi = 29.7 + \ln[(T_\mathrm{e}/10^6\,\mathrm{K})/(n_\mathrm{e}/\mathrm{cm}^{-3})^{1/2}]$, as in 2.1.1 (eqs. 4, 5). Saturation follows [Cowie and McKee](#cowie-mckee-1977) (1977), with $q_\mathrm{sat} = 5\phi_\mathrm{sat}\rho c_\mathrm{s}^3$, $c_\mathrm{s}$ the local sound speed and $\phi_\mathrm{sat} = \sqrt f$ as in the paper of 2016, and with $\sigma = \kappa_\mathrm{Sp}|\nabla T|/q_\mathrm{sat}$ (eqs. 6, 7). **[GK]** As in the paper of 2016 (2.2.8), $\sigma$ is formed from $\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}$, so that the flux saturates at $fx_\mathrm{i}q_\mathrm{sat}$, reduced by $f^{3/2}$ rather than by $\sqrt f$.

With magnetic fields, the classical flux takes the anisotropic form native to PLUTO, $\mathbf{q}_\mathrm{class} = \kappa_\parallel\hat{\mathbf{b}}(\hat{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\nabla T) + \kappa_\perp[\nabla T - \hat{\mathbf{b}}(\hat{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\nabla T)]$, with $\hat{\mathbf{b}} = \mathbf{B}/|\mathbf{B}|$ ([Kooij, Grønnow and Fraternali](#kooij-et-al-2021) 2021, §2.2, eq. 8; [Spitzer](#spitzer-1962) 1962; [Braginskii](#braginskii-1965) 1965). Along the field $\kappa_\parallel = \kappa_\mathrm{Sp}$, and across it $\kappa_\perp = 8(\pi m_\mathrm{H}k_\mathrm{B})^{1/2}n_\mathrm{H}^2e^2c^2\ln\Psi/(3|\mathbf{B}|^2T^{1/2})$, with $m_\mathrm{H}$ the mass and $n_\mathrm{H}$ the number density of hydrogen and $c$ the speed of light (eq. 9). For the parameters of the paper $\kappa_\perp/\kappa_\parallel \lesssim 10^{-9}$, so that $\kappa_\perp$ is set to zero. In these runs anisotropic conduction is either off or at the full Spitzer value, $f = 1$. The calibration gives $f$ between 0.03 and 0.15 for the most realistic orientations of the field (2.8.8). **[GK]** As its authors describe it, the module of PLUTO forms the flux as $q\mathbf{F}_\mathrm{class}/(q + |\mathbf{F}_\mathrm{class}|)$, the harmonic mean of the classical flux of eq. 8 and the saturated flux $q = 5\phi\rho c_\mathrm{iso}^3$, with $c_\mathrm{iso}$ the isothermal sound speed and $\phi$ a parameter of order unity ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012) 2012, §2.1.1, eqs. 6, 7), by default 0.3 ([Vaidya et al.](#vaidya-et-al-2017) 2017, §2). This is the form of eq. 3, with $\phi = \sqrt f$. On the faces the module differences the temperature centrally, the transverse gradient being the mean of four differences as in the asymmetric scheme of 2.3.2, and takes the saturated flux upwind ([Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012) 2012, App. A, eqs. A1 to A4). Limiting of the transverse gradients after 2.3.4 is an option, and conduction is advanced explicitly, by sub-cycling or by super-time-stepping ([Vaidya et al.](#vaidya-et-al-2017) 2017, §3, App. B). The paper does not say which of these options it uses.

#### 2.3.8 Anisotropic conduction for clouds in fast outflows

[Brüggen, Scannapieco and Grete](#bruggen-et-al-2023) (2023, [arXiv:2304.09881](https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.09881), §1) study cold clouds in the hot winds of starbursting galaxies, the fifth paper of a series on how such outflows launch clouds. In the first, [Scannapieco and Brüggen](#scannapieco-bruggen-2015) (2015) found with hydrodynamics and cooling that in a supersonic wind the Mach cone around the cloud suppresses shear instabilities, so that the cloud travels about 40 radii before breaking up. In the second, [Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) (2016) added isotropic conduction at the full Spitzer value: an evaporative layer compresses the cloud and shields it from the wind, delaying its disruption, but makes it so dense that the wind accelerates it to only a small fraction of its own speed, in conflict with observed outflows. The third, by [Cottle et al.](#cottle-et-al-2020) (2020), added strong fields with $\beta = 1$, without conduction: parallel fields barely changed the lifetime, while perpendicular fields squeezed the cloud and raised its loss of mass. [Kooij, Grønnow and Fraternali](#kooij-et-al-2021) (2021) treated only slow winds, with $\chi_0 = 200$ and 75 km s$^{-1}$ (2.3.7, 2.8.8). The fifth paper combines a weak field on large scales, which sets the direction of conduction, with a conductivity of 10 per cent of the Spitzer value, which stands for fields on small scales.

The classical isotropic flux is $\kappa(T)\nabla T$ with $\kappa(T) = f_\mathrm{cond} \times 5.6 \times 10^{-7}\,T^{5/2}$ erg s$^{-1}$ K$^{-1}$ cm$^{-1}$, and the saturated flux, after [Cowie and McKee](#cowie-mckee-1977) (1977), is $0.34n_\mathrm{e}k_\mathrm{B}Tc_\mathrm{s,e}$ along $\nabla T$, with $c_\mathrm{s,e} = (k_\mathrm{B}T/m_\mathrm{e})^{1/2}$ the isothermal sound speed of the electrons ([Brüggen, Scannapieco and Grete](#bruggen-et-al-2023) 2023, §2, eqs. 2, 3). Both enter the energy equation with a positive divergence, so that heat flows down the gradient (§3.1, eq. 12). In the anisotropic case the classical flux is $\kappa(T)\hat{\mathbf{b}}(\hat{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\nabla T)$ and the saturated one $0.34n_\mathrm{e}k_\mathrm{B}Tc_\mathrm{s,e}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\hat{\mathbf{b}}\cdot\nabla T)\,\hat{\mathbf{b}}$ (eqs. 4, 5). The plasma is weakly collisional, since the Larmor radius of the ions is far below their mean free path, so that the magnetic moment of the particles is conserved and changes of the field on small scales go with changes of the perpendicular pressure. Where this anisotropy of pressure exceeds $1/\beta$, the mirror instability produces small fluctuations of the field, which reduce the effective mean free path of the electrons by a factor of about 5 ([Chandran and Cowley](#chandran-cowley-1998) 1998; [Komarov et al.](#komarov-et-al-2016) 2016). The paper models this by reducing the classical coefficient while leaving the saturated flux unchanged, with $f_\mathrm{cond} = 0.1$ to allow for further fluctuations, which also makes the runs ten times cheaper than with $f = 1$. Unlike the paper of 2016 and Kooij et al. (2.2.8, 2.3.7), it thus does not reduce the saturated flux. Electrons and ions are assumed to share one temperature. **[GK]** The saturated flux of Cowie and McKee, $0.4(2k_\mathrm{B}T/\pi m_\mathrm{e})^{1/2}n_\mathrm{e}k_\mathrm{B}T$ (2.2.1), has the coefficient $0.4(2/\pi)^{1/2} = 0.32$ in this form, rather than 0.34.

In units of the cloud-crushing time, $t_\mathrm{cc} = \chi_0^{1/2}R_\mathrm{c}/v_\mathrm{h}$, with $\chi_0$ the initial density contrast, $R_\mathrm{c}$ the radius of the cloud and $v_\mathrm{h}$ the speed of the wind, a radiative cloud depends only on its column density $R_\mathrm{c}n_\mathrm{i,c}$, with $n_\mathrm{i,c}$ the density of ions in the cloud, also with conduction ([Brüggen, Scannapieco and Grete](#bruggen-et-al-2023) 2023, §1, §2, eq. 1). The mean free path of the electrons is $\lambda_\mathrm{i} = 1.3 \times 10^{18}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\,T_7^2/n_\mathrm{i,c}$, with $T_7$ the temperature of the wind in units of $10^7$ K. Below a column density of $1.3 \times 10^{18}\,T_7^2$ cm$^{-2}$ the conducted energy reaches the whole cloud, and above it a shell of volume $4\pi R_\mathrm{c}^2\lambda_\mathrm{i}$. For a high column density the ratio of heating by conduction to cooling in the heated region is then about $0.44\,\Lambda_{-21}^{-1}(\chi_0/1000)^{-1}T_7^{-1/2}$, with $\Lambda_{-21}$ the cooling rate in units of $10^{-21}$ erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$ and $\chi_0 = 1000\,T_7$ in pressure equilibrium (eq. 6), so that cooling wins in a hot wind. For a low column density the ratio grows as $0.44\,\Lambda_{-21}^{-1}(\chi_0/1000)^{-1}T_7^{3/2}(4 \times 10^{18}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}/n_\mathrm{i,c}R_\mathrm{c})$, and the cloud is rapidly disrupted (eqs. 7, 8). The simulated clouds lie above this column density, so that cooling outpaces heating by conduction in all but their outer layers. Numerically, the classical and saturated fluxes are combined by the harmonic mean $\mathbf{q} = q_\mathrm{sat}\mathbf{q}_\mathrm{cl}/(q_\mathrm{sat} + q_\mathrm{cl})$ of [Mignone et al.](#mignone-et-al-2012) (2012), which respects their parabolic and hyperbolic natures, and the transverse gradients of temperature are limited after [Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) (2007), since they are not centred on the faces (§3.1, eq. 15; 2.3.4). 3.4 records how the code at the commit that the paper names differs from this description.

### 2.4 The numerical integration of conduction

#### 2.4.1 The semi-implicit scheme of Vieser and Hensler

Building conduction into an existing hydrodynamic code is complicated by its time step, $\tau_\mathrm{cond} = (e/T\kappa)[\min(\mathrm{d}r, \mathrm{d}z)]^2$, much shorter than the hydrodynamic one where temperatures or conductivities are high ([Vieser and Hensler](#vieser-hensler-2007a) 2007a, App. A, eq. A.1). Vieser and Hensler solve the equation of the internal energy for conduction, $\partial e/\partial t = r^{-1}\partial_r(r\kappa\,\partial_rT) + \partial_z(\kappa\,\partial_zT)$, rewritten for the temperature by the factor $T/e$, in cylindrical coordinates (eqs. A.2, A.3). The two directions are treated separately in the same way. Along $r$, at fixed $z$, the new temperatures of a row are found by an alternating-direction implicit (ADI) method, and once the row has converged the next row follows. The directions are coupled by the fractional steps of [Yanenko](#yanenko-1971) (1971), after the implicit scheme of [Crank and Nicolson](#crank-nicolson-1947) (1947) and [Juncosa and Young](#juncosa-young-1957) (1957) (§3). A purely explicit update would limit the step to the conduction time step, beyond which it is unstable. They therefore weight an implicit part by $0.5 < \alpha < 1$ and an explicit part by $1 - \alpha$, as $(T^{n+1}_{i,j} - T^n_{i,j})/\Delta t = (1 - \alpha)L^n_{i,j} + \alpha L^{n+1}_{i,j}$, with the radial operator in conservative form, $L_{i,j} = [2/(r_{i,j} + r_{i,j+1})](T_{i,j}/e_{i,j})[r_{i,j+1}\kappa_{i,j+1}(T_{i,j+1} - T_{i,j}) - r_{i,j}\kappa_{i,j}(T_{i,j} - T_{i,j-1})]/\Delta r^2$ (eq. A.4). Each row then forms a tridiagonal system (eq. A.5). The weight follows from the ratio $\beta = \tau_\mathrm{hyd}/\tau_\mathrm{cond}$ as $\alpha = (1 - \beta - e^{-\beta})/[\beta(e^{-\beta} - 1)]$, limited to $0.5 \leq \alpha \leq 1$, so that the implicit part dominates where conduction is fast and makes up at least half elsewhere (eqs. A.6 to A.8, Fig. A.1). Since $\kappa$ depends strongly on temperature, the conductivities are updated with the new temperatures and eq. A.4 is solved again, until the temperatures change by less than $10^{-4}$.

They test the method on problems with known solutions, a constant conductivity and $\kappa \propto T^{5/2}$, for which a self-similar solution exists, with results of similar quality, and show the constant case ([Vieser and Hensler](#vieser-hensler-2007a) 2007a, App. A). A spherical conduction front spreads through a static medium of frozen, uniform density, $n = 6.6 \times 10^{-4}$ cm$^{-3}$, on a cylindrical grid of $200 \times 100$ cells with $-0.032 < z < 0.032$ pc and $0 < r < 0.032$ pc. It starts from the analytic solution at 0.5 s, with the conductivity of a plasma at $5.6 \times 10^6$ K. Within the hottest region the relative error is 2 to 3 per cent, somewhat larger at the edge of the front, where the temperature is orders of magnitude lower, and at late times the zero gradient imposed at the boundaries shows (Fig. A.2). The scheme of the paper of 2016 (2.4.2) takes the same weight $\alpha(\beta)$, but linearises the conductivity through the variable $z$ instead of iterating, advances each direction by half a step, and works in Cartesian coordinates.

#### 2.4.2 The semi-implicit scheme of the simulations of 2016

[Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) (2016, App. A) integrate the heat equation, a parabolic partial differential equation, in its two-dimensional Cartesian form $\partial T/\partial t = (T/e)[\partial_x(\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}\,\partial_x T) + \partial_y(\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}\,\partial_y T)]$ (eq. A1), with $e = P/(\gamma - 1)$ the internal energy density and $\gamma = 5/3$. An alternating-direction implicit (ADI) method splits the difference equations by direction. A sweep along $x$, at fixed $y$, is followed by a sweep along $y$ that starts from the new temperatures, each over half the time step. The equation is non-linear, since $\kappa_\mathrm{Sp} \propto T^{5/2}$ (eq. 2), and it is solved with a method for non-linear parabolic equations from [Press et al.](#press-et-al-2007) (2007). To keep the hydrodynamic time step free of the limit of conduction, and the computation short, the time integration is semi-implicit, after [Vieser and Hensler](#vieser-hensler-2007a) (2007a): the implicit part is weighted by a factor $0.5 < \alpha < 1$ and the explicit part by $1 - \alpha$. **[GK]** At constant density $e \propto T$, so that eq. A1 is the energy equation $\partial e/\partial t = \nabla\cdot(\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}\nabla T)$ written for the temperature, and its factor $T/e = (\gamma - 1)/(nk_\mathrm{B})$, with $n$ the density of particles, does not depend on $T$.

In the sweep along $x$, the temperature advances over half a step as $(T^{n+1}_{i,j} - T^n_{i,j})/(\Delta t/2) = \alpha\,(T^{n+1}_{i,j}/e^{n+1}_{i,j})(z^{n+1}_{i+1,j} - 2z^{n+1}_{i,j} + z^{n+1}_{i-1,j})/\Delta x^2 + (1 - \alpha)\,(T^n_{i,j}/e^n_{i,j})[\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}(T^n_{i+1/2,j})(T^n_{i+1,j} - T^n_{i,j}) - \kappa_\mathrm{Sp}(T^n_{i-1/2,j})(T^n_{i,j} - T^n_{i-1,j})]/\Delta x^2$ ([Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) 2016, App. A, eq. A2). The implicit part uses the variable $z = \int\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}\,\mathrm{d}T = (2/7)\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}T$ (eq. A3), in which the flux is linear, and linearises it about the old temperature, $z^{n+1}_{i,j} = (2/7)\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}(T^n_{i,j})T^n_{i,j} + (T^{n+1}_{i,j} - T^n_{i,j})\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}(T^n_{i,j})$ (eq. A4). The explicit part takes the conductivity at the faces of the cells, and App. A does not say how the temperature there is formed. Eqs. A2 to A4 then form a tridiagonal system for the new temperatures of each row, $A_{i,j}T^{n+1}_{i-1,j} + B_{i,j}T^{n+1}_{i,j} + C_{i,j}T^{n+1}_{i+1,j} = D_{i,j}$ (eq. A5), which the Thomas algorithm solves efficiently ([Press et al.](#press-et-al-2007) 2007). The weight $\alpha$ depends on the ratio $\beta = \tau_\mathrm{hydro}/\tau_\mathrm{cond}$ of the hydrodynamic to the conductive time step, $\alpha = (1 - \beta - e^{-\beta})/[\beta(e^{-\beta} - 1)]$, limited to 0.5 from below and to 1 from above (eq. A6). The implicit part thus dominates where the conductive time step is much shorter than the hydrodynamic one, and contributes half or more elsewhere. **[GK]** With this weight, the weighted scheme reproduces exactly the decay of a single mode over one step: for $\mathrm{d}y/\mathrm{d}t = -y/\tau_\mathrm{cond}$, requiring $[1 - (1 - \alpha)\beta]/(1 + \alpha\beta) = e^{-\beta}$ gives eq. A6. As $\beta$ grows from zero to infinity, $\alpha$ rises from 1/2 to 1, so that the limits only guard the formula numerically.

App. A shows the algorithm for eq. 1 ([Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) 2016, App. A). For the flux of eq. 8, the coefficient $f\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}/(1 + \sigma)$ takes the place of $\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}$, treated as a single coefficient that depends on temperature. In the sweep along $x$, $\sigma$ is discretised as $\sigma_{i,j} = \kappa_\mathrm{Sp}(T_{i,j})\,|(T_{i+1,j} - T_{i-1,j})/(2\Delta x)|/(5\Phi_\mathrm{s}\rho_{i,j}c^3_{i,j})$, and analogously along $y$ (eq. A7), so that each sweep measures only the gradient along its own direction. **[GK]** The closed form of $z$ in eq. A3 holds for a coefficient proportional to $T^{5/2}$. The coefficient $f\kappa_\mathrm{Sp}/(1 + \sigma)$ also depends on the gradient, and in the saturated limit it scales as $T^{3/2}/|\nabla T|$, so that eq. A3 then holds only approximately. The paper does not say how it forms $z$ for this coefficient.

#### 2.4.3 The test of a conduction front

The scheme is tested on the propagation of a plane conduction front through a static, uniform medium whose density stays constant ([Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) 2016, App. A). The temperature then follows the analytic solution of [Reale](#reale-1995) (1995), $T = T_\mathrm{c}(1 - x^2/x_\mathrm{f}^2)^{2/5}$ (eq. A8a), with the central temperature $T_\mathrm{c} = 0.6\,(Q^2/at)^{2/9}$ (eq. A8b) and the position of the front $x_\mathrm{f} = 1.01\,(Q^{5/2}at)^{2/9}$ (eq. A8c). Here $a = 5.6 \times 10^{-7}(\gamma - 1)/(k_\mathrm{B}n)$ is a constant, and $Q$, the integral of $T$ over the whole space, is $1.5 \times 10^9$ K pc, which gives a maximum temperature of $2 \times 10^6$ K at $t = 1$ Myr. The run starts from the analytic solution at 1 Myr, for a plasma of particle density $n = 10^{-3}$ cm$^{-3}$. Its grid is 1 kpc long, with 500 cells of 2 pc, the resolution of the reference simulation (2.8.2), and zero gradients of temperature at all boundaries. Fig. A1 compares the numerical and the analytic solutions, with their relative error, at 1, 10, 20, 30, 40 and 50 Myr. They agree very well, with a relative error below 0.01 per cent in the hottest region. Up to 40 Myr, the relative error diverges where the temperature falls to zero, where it is meaningless. At 50 Myr the front has reached the boundary, where the imposed zero gradient makes the two solutions differ visibly, with a relative error of about 70 per cent. **[GK]** The coefficients 0.6 and 1.01 are those of the plane self-similar solution for a conductivity proportional to $T^{5/2}$, normalised so that $Q$ is the integral over the whole line: 0.605 and 1.012. With the parameters of the test they give $T_\mathrm{c} = 2.05 \times 10^6$ K and $x_\mathrm{f} = 444$ pc at 1 Myr. The constant $a$ corresponds to $\kappa_\mathrm{Sp} = 5.6 \times 10^{-7}\,T^{5/2}$, a Coulomb logarithm of 32.9, close to the value of 33.2 that eq. 3 gives at $10^6$ K and $10^{-3}$ cm$^{-3}$. The test exercises the classical conductivity alone, as neither $f$ nor saturation enters it.

#### 2.4.4 The explicit integration of anisotropic conduction

The schemes of [Sharma and Hammett](#sharma-hammett-2007) (2007, §2) are conservative and fully explicit. In two dimensions they update the internal energy as $e^{n+1}_{i,j} = e^n_{i,j} - \Delta t[(q^n_{x,i+1/2,j} - q^n_{x,i-1/2,j})/\Delta x + (q^n_{y,i,j+1/2} - q^n_{y,i,j-1/2})/\Delta y]$, with the fluxes of 2.3 (eq. 3), and the extension to three dimensions is straightforward. Ignoring variations of the density, the time step must satisfy $\Delta t \leq \min(\Delta x^2, \Delta y^2)/[2(\chi_\parallel + \chi_\perp)]$, the condition of stability of [Richtmyer and Morton](#richtmyer-morton-1967) (1967) (eq. 4). The harmonic mean of $n\chi$ on the faces keeps the schemes stable at this step where the density varies, while an arithmetic mean would tighten it by about the ratio of the densities on either side of a face (2.3.2). An implicit generalisation of the schemes could take longer steps, but fast implicit schemes for anisotropic conduction are not simple to construct. **[GK]** For $\chi_\perp = 0$, with a uniform field and density on a grid of square cells, the fastest mode of either centred scheme decays at the rate $4\chi_\parallel/\Delta x^2$, so that eq. 4 is then the exact limit of stability of the explicit update. The condition holds for $\gamma = 2$. For another $\gamma$, $\chi_\parallel$ and $\chi_\perp$ in eq. 4 carry the factor $\gamma - 1$.

#### 2.4.5 Super-time-stepping of conduction

An explicit integration of diffusion is limited by a time step proportional to the square of the cell size, which can make runs with large diffusivities or fine grids prohibitively long ([Brüggen, Scannapieco and Grete](#bruggen-et-al-2023) 2023, §3.1). Their code therefore integrates conduction by the Runge–Kutta–Legendre super-time-stepping of [Meyer, Balsara and Aslam](#meyer-et-al-2014) (2014), in an update split from the MHD. A super-step of $s$ stages is equivalent to $O(s^2)$ explicit diffusive steps, and the scheme of second order, RKL2, takes two such super-steps in each MHD update. The earlier paper of the series with conduction used the general implicit solver of diffusion in Flash instead (§4.4). **[GK]** In the code at the commit that the paper names (3.4), a super-step $\tau$ of half the hydrodynamic step takes $s = \lfloor(\sqrt{9 + 16\tau/\Delta t_\mathrm{diff}} - 1)/2\rfloor + 1$ stages, raised to the next odd number, with $\Delta t_\mathrm{diff}$ the explicit diffusive step. This follows from the stability limit $\tau \leq \Delta t_\mathrm{diff}(s^2 + s - 2)/4$ of RKL2, and the code warns when $2\tau/\Delta t_\mathrm{diff}$ exceeds 200. [Vaidya et al.](#vaidya-et-al-2017) (2017) recommend the same scheme for anisotropic conduction in PLUTO (2.3.7).

### 2.5 Radiative cooling in collisional ionisation equilibrium

#### 2.5.1 Cooling in the energy equation

Radiative cooling and conduction enter the equation of the energy density $e$, $\partial e/\partial t + \nabla\cdot[(e + P)\mathbf{v}] = -\rho^2\Lambda - \nabla\cdot\mathbf{q}$ ([Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) 2016, §2.2, eq. 9). Here $\mathbf{v}$ is the velocity, $\rho$ the density and $P = (\gamma - 1)U$ the pressure, with $U$ the internal energy density and $\gamma = 5/3$. The cooling rate $\Lambda$ depends on temperature and metallicity, and the simulations take it from the tables of [Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) (1993, [1993ApJS...88..253S](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/1993ApJS...88..253S/abstract)) for collisional ionisation equilibrium. **[GK]** With $\Lambda$ in erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$, as Sutherland and Dopita tabulate it and as §4.2 of the paper quotes it, the loss term is a power per unit volume only if $\rho$ there stands for number densities. The paper does not say which number densities multiply $\Lambda$.

#### 2.5.2 The cooling functions of Sutherland and Dopita

[Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) (1993, §1) aim at a full, self-consistent grid of cooling functions over a wide range of temperature and metallicity, for collisional ionisation equilibrium (CIE) and for gas cooling out of equilibrium, and at showing how far the latter departs from the former. Earlier cooling functions were mostly optically thin CIE calculations with older atomic data, such as those of [Cox and Tucker](#cox-tucker-1969) (1969), and the recent ones of [Gaetz and Salpeter](#gaetz-salpeter-1983) (1983), [Böhringer and Hensler](#bohringer-hensler-1989) (1989) and [Landini and Monsignori Fossi](#landini-fossi-1990) (1990) summed the emission only over limited ranges of wavelength. Their new code, MAPPINGS II, extends MAPPINGS ([Binette, Dopita and Tuohy](#binette-et-al-1985) 1985) from 66 to 225 ions of 16 elements (H, He, C, N, O, Ne, Na, Mg, Al, Si, S, Cl, Ar, Ca, Fe and Ni) in all stages of ionisation, and its range of temperature from $\log T \lesssim 5.0$ to $\lesssim 8.5$, above which relativistic effects matter (§2). It follows 1683 radiative transitions, some 940 of them resonance lines, from the fine-structure line [N II] $205\,\mu$m up to a resonance line of Ni XXVIII at 1.24 Å. It computes the continuum of free–free, free–bound and two-photon emission ion by ion, includes non-relativistic Compton heating, and follows the ionising radiation in 360 bins from 5 eV to 11 keV.

The ionisation balance follows from photoionisation, fitted after Seaton with up to several edges per ion and with Auger processes and secondary ionisations; from collisional ionisation by electrons, computed shell by shell from the five-parameter fits of Younger, with the data of [Arnaud and Rothenflug](#arnaud-rothenflug-1985) (1985) and more accurate exponential integrals; from 61 reactions of charge transfer with H and He; and from radiative and dielectronic recombination, fitted after [Aldrovandi and Péquignot](#aldrovandi-pequignot-1973) (1973) with corrections at low temperature for dielectronic recombination ([Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) 1993, §3.1 to §3.4). The recombination of hydrogenic ions, decisive because hydrogen dominates, returns to the less approximate expressions of Seaton, which stay accurate up to $\log(T/Z^2) \approx 7.5$ and then tend to zero, where the usual fit would wrongly let hydrogen recombine above $10^8$ K (§3.4.1, Figs. 1, 2). Resonance lines are excited collisionally from the ground state, without collisional de-excitation, which restricts the results to densities below about $10^8$ cm$^{-3}$; their transfer uses an escape probability (§3.5, §3.5.1). Forbidden lines come from five-level atoms, intersystem and fine-structure lines from two levels, the He-like triplets from three coupled levels, and Fe II from a model of 14 terms (§3.5.2 to §3.5.5). The net cooling function sums the losses by lines, continuum, recombination, collisional ionisation and Compton scattering, less the heating by photoionisation, $\Lambda_\mathrm{net}(T, A) = \Lambda_\mathrm{lines} + \Lambda_\mathrm{cont} \pm \Lambda_\mathrm{rec} - \Lambda_\mathrm{photo} + \Lambda_\mathrm{coll} \pm \Lambda_\mathrm{Compton}$, with $A$ the abundances (§3.7, eq. 48). Recombination enters as a net heating or cooling, from the difference between the mean energy of the recombining electrons and $kT$, so that the free–bound continuum is not summed directly (§3.7.1). The equilibrium itself is found by integrating the time-dependent balance over a span much longer than all atomic time scales, a method more stable for minor species than the inversion of a matrix, and it agrees well with Arnaud and Rothenflug (§4, Figs. 3 to 5).

The CIE functions come from independent, isochoric, stationary slabs at a nominal hydrogen density of 1 cm$^{-3}$, with photoionisation by their own radiation suppressed, for $4.0 \leq \log T \leq 8.5$ in steps of 0.05 dex ([Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) 1993, §5.2). Eight abundance series cover $[\mathrm{Fe/H}]$ = $+0.5$, 0, $-0.5$, $-1.0$, $-1.5$, $-2.0$, $-3.0$ and no metals, with the solar abundances of [Anders and Grevesse](#anders-grevesse-1989) (1989) (§5.1, Table 4). At $[\mathrm{Fe/H}] \leq -1.0$ the ratios between the elements are primordial, with $[\mathrm{O/Fe}] = +0.5$ (§6.1), which matters because oxygen is perhaps the most important coolant; $-0.5$ interpolates between solar and primordial ratios, and $+0.5$ keeps the solar ones. With $n_\mathrm{e}$ the density of electrons, $n_\mathrm{t}$ the total density of ions and $N = n_\mathrm{t} + n_\mathrm{e}$, the tables give the mean particle mass $\bar\mu$, the density $\rho = N\bar\mu$, the pressure $P = NkT$, the internal energy $U = (3/2)NkT$ and the cooling time $\tau_\mathrm{cool} = U/\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$, together with the normalised cooling rate $\Lambda_N = \Lambda_\mathrm{net}/(n_\mathrm{t}n_\mathrm{e})$ and the isothermal sound speed $(\gamma P/\rho)^{1/2}$ with $\gamma = 5/3$ (eqs. 51 to 58). Here $\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$ is a power per unit volume and $\Lambda_N$ is in erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$. The normalisation by $n_\mathrm{t}n_\mathrm{e}$ holds for any composition, unlike the factors $n_\mathrm{H}n_\mathrm{e}$ or $n_\mathrm{e}^2$ common for gas rich in hydrogen. Table 6 lists, for each series, $\log T$, $n_\mathrm{e}$, $n_\mathrm{H}$ and $n_\mathrm{t}$, $\log\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$, $\log\Lambda_N$, $\log U$, $\log\tau_\mathrm{cool}$, $P$, $\rho$, the sound speed and $\bar\mu$, and an erratum printed with it adds 0.176 to the columns of $\log U$ and $\log\tau_\mathrm{cool}$; Table 5 gives the ionisation balance of all elements. For solar abundances, $\log\Lambda_N$ rises from $-23.06$ at $10^4$ K to a broad maximum of about $-20.96$ at $\log T$ = 5.3 to 5.35, after a shoulder of $-20.98$ at 4.95, falls to $-21.96$ at $10^6$ K and $-22.57$ at $10^7$ K, reaches its minimum of $-22.73$ near $10^{7.4}$ K and rises again to $-22.38$ at $10^{8.5}$ K (Table 6, Fig. 8).

CIE gives an upper limit to the cooling rate, since every time-dependent effect reduces the cooling, so that cooling times from CIE are lower limits ([Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) 1993, §6.1). The results agree with those of Gaetz and Salpeter, Böhringer and Hensler, and Landini and Monsignori Fossi. All models converge above $10^{7.5}$ K, where the gas is fully ionised and free–free emission dominates, and its power law can extend the function up to about $10^9$ K. Lines of the iron group dominate the cooling at high temperatures and the light elements C, O and Ne at low ones, and the primordial ratios show mainly in the oxygen peak near $10^{5.3}$ K, relative to the carbon peak near $10^{4.9}$ K. As fitting polynomials to the curves would be inefficient and miss their smaller variations between $10^5$ and $10^6$ K, the authors offer the tables for interpolation, and data files on request (acknowledgements). **[GK]** These definitions settle what 2.5.1 left open: the loss per unit volume is $n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{t}\Lambda_N$, and $\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$ of Table 6 equals it for $n_\mathrm{H} = 1$ cm$^{-3}$. Table 6 also bears on two statements of the paper of 2016 (2.8.4). For solar abundances $n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H}$ is 0.93 at $10^{4.3}$ K and 0.82 at $10^{4.25}$ K, so that above $10^{4.3}$ K less than about 10 per cent of the hydrogen is neutral, as the paper states. Its rate of $10^{-23.93}$ erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$, however, is not the mean between $10^4$ and $10^{4.3}$ K: there the mean of $\log\Lambda_N$ is $-22.15$ and that of $\log\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$ $-23.05$ for solar abundances, and $-22.37$ and $-23.29$ without metals, while the mean of $\log\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$ between $10^4$ and $10^{4.15}$ K comes to $-23.92$.

#### 2.5.3 The cooling curve of SPEX

[Schure et al.](#schure-et-al-2009) (2009, [arXiv:0909.5204](https://arxiv.org/abs/0909.5204), §1, §2) compute a new cooling curve for collisional ionisation equilibrium with the spectral code SPEX, version 2.00.11, which is tailored to the extreme ultraviolet and X-rays and based on the line-emission model mekal with many later updates. It includes 15 elements, H, He, C, N, O, Ne, Na, Mg, Al, Si, S, Ar, Ca, Fe and Ni, with the solar abundances of [Anders and Grevesse](#anders-grevesse-1989) (1989), and 5466 lines against the 1683 of Sutherland and Dopita (2.5.2). The ionisation balance is that of [Arnaud and Rothenflug](#arnaud-rothenflug-1985) (1985), except for iron, and a more complete list of the lines of the Fe L complex raises the cooling near $10^7$ K. The spectra are computed from $10^{3.8}$ to $10^{8.16}$ K in steps of 0.04 dex and integrated from 0.1 eV to 1 MeV. The emissivity $\Lambda_N$, in erg s$^{-1}$ cm$^3$, is normalised to $n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{H}V = 1$ cm$^{-3}$, unlike the normalisation of Sutherland and Dopita by $n_\mathrm{t}n_\mathrm{e}$. For a hydrodynamic code, it is multiplied by the ratio $n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H}$, which SPEX also gives, to $\Lambda_\mathrm{hd} = (n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H})\Lambda_N$, so that the luminosity is $L = \int n_\mathrm{H}^2\Lambda_\mathrm{hd}(T)\,\mathrm{d}V$ (eqs. 1, 2). Table 2 lists $\log\Lambda_N$, $\log\Lambda_\mathrm{hd}$ and $n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H}$, and Table 4 the contribution of each element at solar abundance, from which curves for other compositions follow as $\Lambda_N(T) = \sum_i[n_i/n_i(\mathrm{solar})]\Lambda_N(X_i, T)$, with $n_i$ the abundance of element $X_i$ relative to hydrogen (eq. 3, Fig. 2).

The curve of SPEX lies above those of Sutherland and Dopita and of [MacDonald and Bailey](#macdonald-bailey-1981) (1981), mainly through its more complete lines of Fe L and in the extreme ultraviolet, and the difference is largest between $10^4$ and $10^6$ K ([Schure et al.](#schure-et-al-2009) 2009, §2, Fig. 1). Its $\log\Lambda_N$ peaks at $-20.75$ at $10^{4.96}$ K, from carbon, and again at $-20.75$ at $10^{5.32}$ K, from oxygen, falls to $-21.79$ at $10^6$ K and $-22.46$ at $10^7$ K, reaches its minimum of $-22.70$ near $10^{7.44}$ K, and rises to $-22.48$ at $10^{8.16}$ K (Table 2). A peak of hydrogen at $-21.32$ near $10^{4.2}$ K exceeds the value of Sutherland and Dopita at that temperature by about 0.5 dex. The curve of MacDonald and Bailey, used for comparison, joins the rates of [Raymond, Cox and Smith](#raymond-et-al-1976) (1976) between $10^6$ and $10^8$ K to the time-dependent rates of [Shapiro and Moore](#shapiro-moore-1976) (1976) between $10^4$ and $10^6$ K, for gas that starts in equilibrium at $10^6$ K and becomes overionised as it cools faster than it recombines, and extrapolates towards zero at 100 K.

CIE holds for optically thin gas dominated by collisions that cools more slowly than it ionises and recombines ([Schure et al.](#schure-et-al-2009) 2009, §3). Photoionisation, or cooling faster than recombination, overionises the gas, and heating faster than ionisation, as in young supernova remnants, underionises it. Overionised plasmas cool less than in CIE, as found by Shapiro and Moore and by Sutherland and Dopita (2.6.1), and so does intergalactic gas exposed to photoionisation ([Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) 2009), while neutral gas heated by a shock cools much faster at first, as it ionises H and He. Without full radiative transfer, CIE is a reasonable assumption for circumstellar and other low-density gas, except where photoionisation is strong or shocks exceed about 150 km s$^{-1}$, which heat the gas above $10^{5.7}$ K. The rates depend on density only through the factor $n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{H}$, since recombination, collisional ionisation and cooling scale alike, as long as the gas is optically thin.

SPEX includes continuum but no lines beyond 2000 Å, so that it may underestimate the cooling below $hc/\lambda k \approx 10^{4.86}$ K ([Schure et al.](#schure-et-al-2009) 2009, §4). Below $10^4$ K the curve follows instead the rates of [Dalgarno and McCray](#dalgarno-mccray-1972) (1972): excitation of C$^+$, Si$^+$, Fe$^+$, O, O$^+$, N and S$^+$ by electrons, in proportion to $(n_i/n_\mathrm{H})f_\mathrm{i}$, with $f_\mathrm{i} = n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H}$ the ionisation fraction, excitation of Si$^+$ and Fe$^+$ by neutral hydrogen, and the excitation of hydrogen by electrons (eqs. 4 to 13). The ionisation fraction is a free parameter, which depends on shocks, radiation and history, and Table 3 gives the cooling for $f_\mathrm{i}$ = $10^{-4}$, $10^{-3}$, $10^{-2}$ and $10^{-1}$ between 10 and $10^4$ K. The value $10^{-4}$ agrees best with the rates of [Smith, Sigurdsson and Abel](#smith-et-al-2008) (2008) for solar abundances. **[GK]** Eqs. 5 and 6 print three exponents with a positive sign, $e^{63600/T}$, $e^{2694/T}$ and $e^{3496/T}$, which would make the rates diverge as the temperature falls. The cooling of Table 3 decreases steadily towards low temperatures, which implies negative exponents, as for the other terms.

As a test, the grid-adaptive code AMRVAC ([Keppens et al.](#keppens-et-al-2003) 2003), with the HLLC solver, follows the circumstellar medium of a massive star whose fast wind as a Wolf–Rayet star sweeps up the slow wind of its earlier phase as a red supergiant ([Schure et al.](#schure-et-al-2009) 2009, §5). The grid is axisymmetric in a meridional plane over an angle of $\pi$, with reflecting boundaries, a radial extent of about 3 pc and a base of $60 \times 48$ cells refined in 4 to 7 levels, at most $1.56 \times 10^{15}$ cm by $0.0156^\circ$. The red supergiant loses $1.54 \times 10^{-5}\,M_\odot$ yr$^{-1}$ at 4.7 km s$^{-1}$ and $10^3$ K, the Wolf–Rayet star $9.7 \times 10^{-6}\,M_\odot$ yr$^{-1}$ at $1.7 \times 10^3$ km s$^{-1}$ and $10^4$ K, and random perturbations of the density of 1 per cent seed the instabilities. At each step the temperature gives the cooling rate by interpolation in the table, and the loss per unit volume, $\Lambda_\mathrm{hd}n_\mathrm{H}^2$, is subtracted from the thermal energy only, which keeps errors of precision from producing a negative energy (eq. 14). Without cooling the swept-up shell is compressed by a factor of 4, as behind a strong shock with $\gamma = 5/3$. With cooling it becomes much thinner, and above a compression of about 21 it becomes subject to the thin-shell instability of Vishniac. The gas of the shell stays below $10^5$ K, above which it would also become thermally unstable, where $\mathrm{d}\ln\Lambda/\mathrm{d}\ln T < 2$. After about 1750 yr of the Wolf–Rayet phase the instability develops with both cooling curves, and the curve of SPEX, with more cooling above $10^4$ K, compresses the region of shocked Wolf–Rayet wind more and removes more thermal energy (Figs. 3, 4). The curve of MacDonald and Bailey, used in ZEUS-3D, cools more below $10^4$ K through its extrapolation, which makes the shell of the red supergiant patchier. The thickness of the shell is set by the resolution rather than by the curve: finer grids give thinner shells and shorter wavelengths of the instability, while the thermal losses converge (Fig. 5). After about 22,700 yr the shell is highly irregular (Fig. 6).

### 2.6 Cooling out of equilibrium and under radiation

#### 2.6.1 Non-equilibrium cooling of Sutherland and Dopita

The non-equilibrium (NEQ) models of [Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) (1993, §5.3) follow a slab of plasma that starts in CIE at an initial temperature $T_0$ and cools approximately isobarically. A Lagrangian parcel moves through the cooling flow, mediated by the Rankine–Hugoniot equations: $\rho v = \rho_0v_0$, $\rho v^2 + p = \rho_0v_0^2 + p_0$ and $N\,\mathrm{d}H/\mathrm{d}t = -n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{t}\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$, with $H$ the enthalpy per particle (eqs. 59 to 62). Momentum is thus conserved, and departures from isobaric conditions come only from the ram pressure $\rho v^2$. The geometry is an infinite plane-parallel sheet, whose diffuse radiation splits equally upstream and downstream. As the upstream photons have $h\nu < kT$ everywhere and matter less, the ionisation balance couples only to the downstream field, the case of maximal radiation that the authors call full-field. Switching this coupling off gives the optically thin, zero-field case. The resonance lines see the velocity gradient across each step through an escape probability. The calculation stops at $10^4$ K, or earlier where heating by photoionisation comes to dominate and $\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$ turns negative. **[GK]** As printed, eq. 61 multiplies $n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{t}$ by $\Lambda_\mathrm{net}$, which is already a power per unit volume; the normalised rate $\Lambda_N$ makes it balance. Eq. 62 likewise writes $H = mv^2/2 + (5/2)kT/m$, mixing an energy per particle with an energy per unit mass, where the enthalpy per particle is $mv^2/2 + (5/2)kT$.

Three pressures test the effect of density, for $T_0 = 10^{6.5}$ K ([Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) 1993, §5.3.1, Fig. 10): $\log(p/k) \approx 4$, as in galactic fountains, about 6, as in cluster cooling flows, and about 8, where collisional de-excitation matters, with $p/k$ in cm$^{-3}$ K. The functions do not depend on pressure between 4 and 6, while at 8 the de-excitation of forbidden lines depresses the cooling below $10^{4.5}$ K. All further models take $\log(p/k) \approx 6$, the limit of low density. A series of initial temperatures, $10^{5.5}$ to $10^{8.5}$ K in steps of 1 dex, for solar abundances, tests the thermal history (§5.3.2, Figs. 11, 12, Tables 7, 8). The full field changes the total function only slightly, by keeping the gas a little more ionised at a given temperature, a suppression of about 0.1 dex. The zero-field functions follow CIE closely above $10^{6.5}$ K, in agreement with [Shapiro and Moore](#shapiro-moore-1976) (1976), but deviate progressively below $10^6$ K, where the twin peaks of oxygen and carbon are suppressed by a factor of 3 to 10, leaving a nearly featureless curve. With pressure and initial temperature of minor effect, composition remains the key variable, and the abundance series of 2.5.2 is computed for both fields at $\log(p/k) \approx 6$ and $T_0 = 10^{7.5}$ K, typical of the coronae of large clusters, where in CIE all atoms but Fe and Ni are fully ionised (§5.3.3, Figs. 13, 14, Tables 9, 10). The tables give the same quantities as for CIE.

Out of equilibrium the plasma cools less efficiently, since the strong lines of the light elements C, O and Ne are suppressed while the features of iron change little ([Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) 1993, §6.2). The carbon and oxygen peaks near $10^5$ K are greatly reduced or absent, while the iron peaks near $10^6$ and $10^7$ K survive. The ionisation lags behind CIE, chiefly because the hydrogenic and helium-like ions, and light elements in general, recombine slowly (§6.2.1). An ion equilibrates on the time $\tau_\mathrm{CIE} = 1/(\alpha_\mathrm{rec} + S_\mathrm{coll})$, with $\alpha_\mathrm{rec}$ the total rate of recombination and $S_\mathrm{coll}$ that of collisional ionisation, longest for closed shells such as C V, Fe XVII and Fe XXV (eq. 63, Fig. 15). The ions of iron above $10^6$ K equilibrate much faster than the gas cools, but C V and C VI may take longer, so that the slowly recombining C V governs the carbon balance. A significant fraction of C V then survives below $10^{4.5}$ K, the strong coolant C III is reduced at $10^5$ K by a factor of 10 to 100, and by the time it peaks the electrons are too cold to excite its resonance lines (Fig. 16). Any radiation field retards recombination, so that the full-field models are always the most ionised. Lighter ions and lower stages peak at lower temperatures than in CIE: C IV and Si II to Si IV, observed in absorption in the Galactic halo, deviate strongly, N V and O VI less, so that temperatures inferred from these ions under CIE may be grossly in error (Fig. 17). Resonance lines dominate the cooling between $10^{4.5}$ and $10^7$ K, collisional excitation of hydrogen, forbidden lines and Fe II below, where forbidden lines matter more out of equilibrium, and free–free emission above $10^7$ K; above $10^{5.7}$ K CIE and NEQ differ little (§6.2.2, Fig. 18). Even the NEQ functions violate the isobaric criterion of stability of [Schwarz, McCray and Stein](#schwarz-et-al-1972) (1972), $\mathrm{d}\ln\Lambda_N/\mathrm{d}\ln T > 2$, above $10^5$ K, so that thermal instabilities should form clumps of cool gas in hot gas, which intercept less of the diffuse field (§6.2.3, eq. 64). The zero-field functions may then be the most appropriate for simple calculations. For primordial gas, likely close to zero-field in forming galaxies and clusters, non-equilibrium lowers the peaks of helium and hydrogen without broadening them (§6.2.4, Fig. 19).

The cooling of a slab thus depends on its thermal history and on the geometry that sets its coupling to radiation ([Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) 1993, §7). Below $[\mathrm{Fe/H}] = -3.0$ the functions become virtually indistinguishable from those without metals. CIE gives an upper limit to the cooling rates, and the slow recombination of the inefficient helium-like coolants lengthens the cooling time in all NEQ models. Gas heated by shocks should show the opposite, with low stages persisting at high temperature and cooling faster than in CIE over a time of collisional ionisation. This hysteresis between heating and cooling rules out a general cooling function for gas that both heats and cools, so that simulations would need to follow the thermal and ionisation history. Without a local radiation field the cooling becomes independent of the initial temperature, for NEQ only above $10^{6.5}$ K. Where the radiation field stays low, the zero-field NEQ functions may be accurate enough, and where it can build up, the full-field ones give a limiting case.

#### 2.6.2 Time-dependent ionisation of Gnat and Sternberg

[Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) (2007, [arXiv:astro-ph/0608181](https://arxiv.org/abs/astro-ph/0608181), §1, §2) recompute the equilibrium and non-equilibrium cooling efficiencies and ionisation states of dust-free, optically thin gas between $10^4$ and $10^8$ K without external radiation, for H, He, C, N, O, Ne, Mg, Si, S and Fe in all stages of ionisation. The gas cools at constant density (isochoric) or constant pressure (isobaric) from an equilibrium state hotter than $5 \times 10^6$ K, with metallicities $Z$ from $10^{-3}$ to 2 times solar. Earlier work by [Kafatos](#kafatos-1973) (1973) and [Shapiro and Moore](#shapiro-moore-1976) (1976) treated isochoric cooling at one metallicity, Sutherland and Dopita isobaric cooling with few ion fractions (2.6.1), and [Schmutzler and Tscharnuter](#schmutzler-tscharnuter-1993) (1993) isochoric cooling with ion fractions only at solar metallicity. The fraction $x_i$ of element $m$ in stage $i$, with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, changes by collisional ionisation and by radiative and dielectronic recombination with electrons, at rate coefficients $q_i$ and $\alpha_i$, and by charge transfer with H and He, the photoionisation rates being set to zero (eqs. 1, 2). The atomic data are the compilations of the codes ION and Cloudy ([Ferland et al.](#ferland-et-al-1998) 1998). The solar abundances are those of [Asplund, Grevesse and Sauval](#asplund-et-al-2005) (2005), with an enhanced neon abundance and a primordial helium abundance $n_\mathrm{He}/n_\mathrm{H} = 1/12$ for all $Z$ (Table 1). Dust is assumed destroyed by sputtering before the gas cools, as it is above about $3 \times 10^6$ K ([Smith et al.](#smith-et-al-1996) 1996).

Heat lost by the thermal electrons gives $\mathrm{d}Q = \mathrm{d}U + P\,\mathrm{d}V = (3/2 + s)(Nk_\mathrm{B}\,\mathrm{d}T + k_\mathrm{B}T\,\mathrm{d}N)$, with $s = 0$ for isochoric and $s = 1$ for isobaric cooling, and $\mathrm{d}Q = -n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{H}\Lambda(T, x_i, Z)V\,\mathrm{d}t$ ([Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) 2007, §2.2, eqs. 3, 4). The electron cooling efficiency $\Lambda$, in erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$, counts recombination by the kinetic energy of the recombining electrons alone, not by the ionisation energy released, and includes the energy removed by collisional ionisation, collisional excitation followed by line emission, and bremsstrahlung. In the coronal limit it does not depend on density. The temperature then falls as $\mathrm{d}T/\mathrm{d}t = -n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{H}\Lambda/[(3/2 + s)nk_\mathrm{B}] - (T/N)\,\mathrm{d}N/\mathrm{d}t$, with $n$ the total particle density, more slowly for isobaric cooling through the work $P\,\mathrm{d}V$ (eq. 5). For fully ionised hydrogen and helium the second term vanishes and the rate is $n\Lambda/[4.34(3/2 + s)k_\mathrm{B}]$, and eq. 5 can also be written $(3/2)\,\mathrm{d}P/\mathrm{d}t - (5/2)(P/\rho)\,\mathrm{d}\rho/\mathrm{d}t = -n_\mathrm{e}n_\mathrm{H}\Lambda$, with $\rho = \mu n$ and $\mu = (16/27)m_\mathrm{H}$ (eqs. 6, 7). All rates scale as $n^2$, so that $x_i$ as a function of $T$, and departures from equilibrium, do not depend on density or pressure, and the metallicity remains the only free parameter. Departures appear when the cooling time $t_\mathrm{c} = T/|\mathrm{d}T/\mathrm{d}t| \simeq 4.34(3/2 + s)k_\mathrm{B}T/(n\Lambda)$ falls below the recombination time $t_{\mathrm{r},i} \simeq 1/(n\alpha_i^\mathrm{tot})$ (eqs. 8, 9). They are larger for isochoric cooling, which is faster, and for higher $Z$, which cools faster, and the helium-like ions, slow to recombine, persist longest.

The 103 coupled ordinary differential equations are advanced in small steps of pressure, $\Delta P = \varepsilon P$, for isochoric cooling, or of density, $\Delta\rho = \varepsilon\rho$, for isobaric cooling, with $\varepsilon \lesssim 0.05$ ([Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) 2007, §2.3). For each step, Cloudy gives $\Lambda$ from the current non-equilibrium fractions, eq. 7 gives the time interval $\Delta t$, and the solver LSODE of [Hindmarsh](#hindmarsh-1983) (1983), which switches between stiff and non-stiff methods, integrates eq. 1 over $\Delta t$ with the pressure or density varying linearly in time. The local errors of the fractions are held below $10^{-6}$, $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-4}$ for hydrogen, helium and the metals, the integration stops at $10^4$ K, and repeating it with smaller $\varepsilon$ confirms convergence. The equilibrium fractions come from integrating eq. 1 to late times at fixed temperature, which agrees with the algebraic solution of Cloudy and ION.

Whether a cloud cools isochorically or isobarically depends on $t_\mathrm{c}/t_\mathrm{d} \simeq 4.34(3/2 + s)(k_\mathrm{B}T)^{3/2}/(\mu^{1/2}n\Lambda D)$, with the dynamical time $t_\mathrm{d} = D/c_\mathrm{s}$ of a cloud of diameter $D$ and $c_\mathrm{s} = (k_\mathrm{B}T/\mu)^{1/2}$ ([Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) 2007, §3, eqs. 10, 11). Cooling is isobaric for $t_\mathrm{c} \gg t_\mathrm{d}$, at high temperature, and isochoric for $t_\mathrm{c} \ll t_\mathrm{d}$, for large clouds. Isochoric cooling remains isochoric, since $t_\mathrm{c}/t_\mathrm{d} \propto T^{3/2}/\Lambda$ falls as the gas cools. An isobaric cloud contracts as $D = D_0(T/T_0)^{1/3}$ with $n = n_0T_0/T$, so that $t_\mathrm{c}/t_\mathrm{d} \propto T^{13/6}/\Lambda$ falls fast and the cloud turns isochoric at the column density $D_\mathrm{tr}n \simeq 10k_\mathrm{B}^{3/2}\mu^{-1/2}T_\mathrm{tr}^{3/2}/\Lambda(T_\mathrm{tr}) \simeq 40\,T_\mathrm{6,tr}^{2.04}$ pc cm$^{-3}$ for $Z = 1$, with $T_6 = T/10^6$ K (eq. 12, Fig. 1). For an initial state $D_0$, $P_0$, $T_0$ this gives $D_0P_0/T_0^{1/3} \simeq 10k_\mathrm{B}^{5/2}\mu^{-1/2}T_\mathrm{tr}^{13/6}/\Lambda(T_\mathrm{tr}) \simeq 4 \times 10^7\,T_\mathrm{6,tr}^{2.7}$ pc cm$^{-3}$ K $(10^6\,\mathrm{K})^{-1/3}$, an implicit relation for $T_\mathrm{tr}$ (eq. 13). Both use the equilibrium efficiency, as non-equilibrium changes it only by factors of a few. A condensation of 10 kpc with $n = 10^{-5}$ cm$^{-3}$ at $10^6$ K, a column of 0.1 pc cm$^{-3}$ against the transitional 40, thus cools isobarically and turns isochoric at about $10^5$ K for $Z = 1$, or $4 \times 10^4$ K for $Z < 10^{-2}$. **[GK]** With $\Lambda = 2.3 \times 10^{-19}\,T^{-0.54}$ erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$ (eq. 14) and $\mu = (16/27)m_\mathrm{H}$, eqs. 12 and 13 give 39.9 pc cm$^{-3}$ and $4.0 \times 10^7$, and their coefficient 10 is $4.34(3/2 + s)$ for $s = 1$.

Out of equilibrium the gas stays overionised, as recombination lags behind cooling, below about $10^6$ K for $Z = 1$ ([Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) 2007, §4, Fig. 2, Tables 2 to 4). Hydrogen, half ionised at $1.6 \times 10^4$ K in equilibrium and 99.9 per cent neutral at $10^4$ K, is only 9 per cent neutral at $10^4$ K in isochoric cooling. The helium-like ions persist longest: $x(\mathrm{C}^{4+})$ stays above 0.2 down to about $1.4 \times 10^4$ K in isochoric cooling, where in equilibrium it vanishes below $8 \times 10^4$ K. $\mathrm{O}^{5+}$ stays above 10 per cent of its peak of 0.11 at $3.0 \times 10^5$ K down to $2.0 \times 10^4$ K in isochoric and $1.0 \times 10^5$ K in isobaric cooling, where in equilibrium it vanishes below about $2 \times 10^5$ K. Many ions, such as $\mathrm{C}^{++}$, $\mathrm{C}^{3+}$ and $\mathrm{Si}^{3+}$, have two peaks, as dielectronic recombination weakens and radiative recombination takes over at lower temperatures. The equilibrium fractions agree closely with those of Sutherland and Dopita, and the remaining differences come from rate coefficients and abundances, such as their solar O/H of $8.5 \times 10^{-4}$ against $4.6 \times 10^{-4}$ here. For $Z = 2$, $\mathrm{O}^{5+}$ persists down to $10^4$ K. Below $Z \approx 10^{-2}$ the metals no longer affect the cooling, and the fractions tend to universal primordial forms, of which some, such as $\mathrm{O}^{++}$, never reach equilibrium at any $Z$ (Fig. 7). **[GK]** For isobaric cooling the text gives $2.3 \times 10^5$ K as the temperature down to which $x(\mathrm{C}^{4+}) > 0.2$, but in Fig. 2 this ion stays above 0.2 down to about $2 \times 10^4$ K, so $2.3 \times 10^4$ K is presumably meant.

In equilibrium, for $Z \gtrsim 0.1$, resonance lines of metal ions excited by electrons dominate between $10^5$ and about $10^7$ K, and bremsstrahlung above ([Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) 2007, §5, Fig. 8, Tables 13 to 15). Lyman $\alpha$ of hydrogen makes a peak near $2 \times 10^4$ K, followed by peaks of carbon, oxygen, neon and iron at $1.0 \times 10^5$, $3.0 \times 10^5$, $5.0 \times 10^5$ and $1.5 \times 10^6$ K, and for $Z = 1$ the maximum is $4.6 \times 10^{-22}$ erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$ at $2.3 \times 10^5$ K. For $Z \lesssim 0.01$ hydrogen and helium dominate, with a peak of He$^+$ Lyman $\alpha$ near $10^5$ K. For $Z = 1$ between $10^5$ and $10^8$ K, $\Lambda_\mathrm{eq} \approx 2.3 \times 10^{-19}\,T^{-0.54}$ erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$, within a factor of 2.4, or 1.7 below $6.3 \times 10^7$ K (eq. 14). For $Z \gtrsim 1$ between $10^5$ and $10^7$ K, $\Lambda$ is proportional to $Z$, and the isochoric cooling time is about $0.22\,T_6^{1.54}/(nZ)$ Myr, 1.6 times longer for isobaric cooling. In the limit of low $Z$, $\Lambda_\mathrm{eq} \approx 1.7 \times 10^{-16}\,T^{-1.29}$ between $8 \times 10^4$ and $8 \times 10^5$ K and $3.8 \times 10^{-26}\,T^{0.34}$ up to $10^8$ K, within a factor of 1.35 (eq. 15). Out of equilibrium the cooling departs by more than 5 per cent from equilibrium below about $10^6$ K for $Z = 1$, at higher temperatures for higher $Z$, where the isochoric and isobaric curves also part. The peaks are smeared out, as ions persist over wider ranges of temperature, and the efficiencies are suppressed by factors of 2 to 4, as the overionised gas holds fewer of the coolants effective at each temperature, with more energetic transitions that the electrons excite less. For $Z = 1$ the maximum falls to $2.6 \times 10^{-22}$ erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$ at $2.1 \times 10^5$ K, 1.8 times below equilibrium, and isochoric cooling falls below isobaric. A fit to the non-equilibrium efficiency, $5.6 \times 10^{-20}\,T^{-0.46}$ erg cm$^3$ s$^{-1}$, holds within a factor of 2.2 between $10^5$ and $10^8$ K, or 1.5 below $5.5 \times 10^7$ K (eq. 16). At low $Z$ departures occur only below $2 \times 10^5$ K. The suppressed cooling lengthens the cooling time, which in turn limits the departure from equilibrium. These results agree well with those of Sutherland and Dopita (2.6.1) and of Schmutzler and Tscharnuter.

The column densities of ions, $N_i = A_mx_i(T)N_\mathrm{H}$, make ratios that probe temperature and, out of equilibrium, metallicity ([Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) 2007, §6, Figs. 9, 10). In equilibrium the ratios of C IV, N V and O VI depend on temperature alone, but in cooling gas they also depend on $Z$ and stay confined to a narrower range. A cloud with $N_\mathrm{N\,V}/N_\mathrm{O\,VI} \simeq 3$ and $N_\mathrm{C\,IV}/N_\mathrm{O\,VI} \simeq 30$, impossible in equilibrium, matches isochoric cooling at about $1.3 \times 10^5$ K, and ratios near 1 that would indicate $2 \times 10^5$ K in equilibrium occur at much lower temperatures in cooling gas. Many absorbers of high-velocity clouds observed by [Fox et al.](#fox-et-al-2005) (2005) lie near the trajectory for $Z = 1$, though conductive interfaces and turbulent mixing layers remain possible explanations. The optically thin assumption holds up to critical columns of hydrogen at which the cooling at the centre of a slab differs by 50 per cent from that at its edge (App., Fig. A11). These are about $10^{17}$ cm$^{-2}$ below $1.5 \times 10^4$ K, $4 \times 10^{20}$ cm$^{-2}$ near $2 \times 10^4$ K and $5 \times 10^{19}$ cm$^{-2}$ at $2.5 \times 10^4$ K, where trapped recombination radiation changes the Lyman $\alpha$ cooling, rising sharply above $6 \times 10^4$ K. They are $1.5 \times 10^{22}$ cm$^{-2}$ at $1.6 \times 10^5$ K for $Z < 10^{-2}$, through He$^+$ Lyman $\alpha$, and about $10^{21}$ cm$^{-2}$ between $10^6$ and $5 \times 10^6$ K for $Z \gtrsim 1$, where metal lines photoionise O$^{7+}$ and iron, and above $10^{24}$ cm$^{-2}$ elsewhere. Trapping of the recombination radiation of hydrogen and helium in clouds up to $10^{24}$ cm$^{-2}$, by switching from case A to case B, changes the cooling for $Z = 1$ little, and for $Z = 10^{-3}$ by up to a factor of 2, but the ion fractions in both cases only slightly (Fig. A12).

#### 2.6.3 Cooling under photoionisation, element by element

[Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) (2009, [arXiv:0807.3748](https://arxiv.org/abs/0807.3748), abstract, §1) relax two common restrictions of cooling rates, collisional ionisation equilibrium and solar relative abundances, for optically thin gas in ionisation equilibrium exposed to the metagalactic ultraviolet and X-ray background. In CIE the ionisation depends on temperature alone and the cooling scales as the density squared, so that tables in temperature and metallicity suffice. Radiation breaks this: for gas of primordial composition, [Efstathiou](#efstathiou-1992) (1992) found that photoionisation suppresses the cooling between $10^4$ and $10^5$ K by a large factor, but photoionisation of heavy elements had usually been ignored. Densities are expressed as the number density of hydrogen, $n_\mathrm{H} \approx 1.9 \times 10^{-7}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-3}(1 + \delta)(1 + z)^3X_\mathrm{H}/0.752$, with $\delta$ the baryonic overdensity and $X_\mathrm{H}$ the mass fraction of hydrogen, for the cosmological parameters of [Komatsu et al.](#komatsu-et-al-2009) (2009) (eq. 1).

All rates come from grids of the photoionisation code Cloudy, version 07.02 ([Ferland et al.](#ferland-et-al-1998) 1998), for dust-free, optically thin gas in ionisation equilibrium, exposed to the cosmic microwave background and the model of [Haardt and Madau](#haardt-madau-2001) (2001) for the background from galaxies and quasars ([Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) 2009, §2). They are tabulated in $\log n_\mathrm{H}$, $\log T$ and redshift $z$. The contribution $\Lambda_i$ of an element heavier than helium, a rate per unit volume, is the difference between the cooling with all elements at solar abundance and that with element $i$ removed, a valid approximation as long as the element adds little to the electron density, as holds for metals at $Z \lesssim Z_\odot$. Hydrogen and helium are tabulated together, in $\log n_\mathrm{H}$, $\log T$, $z$ and $n_\mathrm{He}/n_\mathrm{H}$, as they set the electron density. Since the metal lines come from collisions of ions with free electrons, $\Lambda_i \propto n_\mathrm{e}n_i$, and the total becomes $\Lambda = \Lambda_\mathrm{H,He} + [(n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H})/(n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H})_\odot]\sum_{i>\mathrm{He}}\Lambda_{i,\odot}10^{[i/\mathrm{H}]}$, with $10^{[i/\mathrm{H}]}$ the abundance of element $i$ relative to solar (eqs. 2 to 4). Without the factor of electron density, the simpler scaling of eq. 3 errs two to three times more at metallicities above about $3Z_\odot$. Where only the metallicity is known, the combined metals at solar ratios replace the sum, as $\Lambda_{Z,\odot}(n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H})/(n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H})_\odot \times Z/Z_\odot$ (eq. 5). The tables hold $\log\Lambda_{i,\odot}/n_\mathrm{H}^2$ for $-8.0 \leq \log n_\mathrm{H} \leq 0.0$ in steps of 0.1 and $2.00 \leq \log T \leq 9.00$ in steps of 0.02, and for hydrogen and helium also $n_\mathrm{e}/n_\mathrm{H}$ and the mean particle mass, at seven values of $n_\mathrm{He}/n_\mathrm{H}$ from 0.0787 to 0.107, which correspond to helium mass fractions $X_\mathrm{He}/(X_\mathrm{H} + X_\mathrm{He})$ from 0.238 to 0.298. They also give the temperature as a function of density and internal energy per unit mass, for codes that evolve the latter, all at 49 redshifts from 0 to 9, in HDF5 files of more than 232 MB, or 61 MB at half the resolution in density and temperature. The default solar abundances of Cloudy are listed in Table 1.

With the eleven elements H, He, C, N, O, Ne, Mg, Si, S, Ca and Fe, eq. 4 reproduces the full calculation at $z = 0$ with median errors in the net rate of 0.33, 1.6 and 6.1 per cent for $Z/Z_\odot$ = 0.1, 1 and 10, and with smaller errors at higher redshift, where Compton cooling off the cosmic microwave background matters more ([Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) 2009, §2). Excluding temperatures within 0.1 dex of thermal equilibrium, where the net rate is a small difference of heating and cooling, the largest errors are 32, 29 and 39 per cent. In CIE, Cloudy agrees with [Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) (2007), Sutherland and Dopita and MAPPINGS III to typically better than 0.3 dex for the same abundances, the largest differences lying near $10^5$ K, where MAPPINGS cools more (Fig. 1). The CIE rates are thus known to better than a factor of 2.

In photoionisation equilibrium the cooling no longer scales as the density squared ([Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) 2009, §3, Fig. 2). For the background at $z = 3$, with a hydrogen photoionisation rate of $1.1 \times 10^{-12}$ s$^{-1}$ and an ionisation parameter $U = 1.6 \times 10^{-5}/n_\mathrm{H}$, the peaks of collisional excitation vanish as the density falls, and at the lowest densities Compton cooling off the cosmic microwave background, $\Lambda \propto n_\mathrm{e}T$, dominates and the equilibrium temperature exceeds $10^4$ K. The net cooling time is $t_\mathrm{cool} = T/|\mathrm{d}T/\mathrm{d}t| = (3/2)nkT/|\Lambda_\mathrm{heat} - \Lambda_\mathrm{cool}|$ at fixed density, a heating time below the equilibrium temperature (eq. 6, Fig. 3). For primordial gas, radiation removes the peaks of collisional excitation of neutral hydrogen and singly ionised helium and so the cooling between $10^4$ and $10^5$ K, raising the cooling time at $10^5$ K by at least an order of magnitude in diffuse intergalactic gas. Metals shorten the cooling times by about an order of magnitude below $10^7$ K, with or without radiation, while above $10^7$ K bremsstrahlung dominates. Radiation suppresses the cooling by metals for the same reason as for hydrogen, by ionising the gas beyond its collisional state. For densities typical of diffuse intergalactic gas, $n_\mathrm{H} \ll 10^{-3}$ cm$^{-3}$ at $z = 3$, it reduces the cooling below $10^6$ K, by more than an order of magnitude below $3 \times 10^5$ K, and above $10^{-3}$ cm$^{-3}$ only below $3 \times 10^4$ K. The ionisation parameter decides, so that a stronger field near sources reaches higher densities. At the lowest densities metals raise the equilibrium temperature by photoheating, through oxygen and iron. Overionisation by rapid cooling suppresses the cooling in the same way (2.6.1, 2.6.2).

For the warm–hot intergalactic medium at $z = 0$, with a photoionisation rate of $8 \times 10^{-14}$ s$^{-1}$ and $U = 9 \times 10^{-7}/n_\mathrm{H}$, the background raises the overdensity needed to cool within a Hubble time from below 10 to about $10^2$ for primordial gas below $10^5$ K, and by about an order of magnitude below $10^5$ K for solar metallicity ([Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) 2009, §4, Figs. 4, 5). Raising the metallicity from zero to solar lowers it by about an order of magnitude over all temperatures of this medium. Ten times the intensity shifts the contour to higher densities, and a harder spectrum at the same hydrogen ionisation rate inhibits cooling above the equilibrium temperature more, since the metal ions excited by collisions hold electrons of higher ionisation energy, while it shortens the heating times below. In CIE the cooling is dominated, from $10^7$ down to $10^4$ K, successively by iron, neon, oxygen, carbon and hydrogen, with bremsstrahlung above $10^7$ K, and where a single element dominates, as oxygen near $2 \times 10^5$ K and iron near $10^6$ K, the rates follow its abundance (§5, Fig. 6). Photoionisation, shown for $n_\mathrm{H} = 10^{-4}$ cm$^{-3}$ at $z = 3$, raises the share of oxygen, lowers those of carbon, helium and especially hydrogen, and shifts the peaks of the elements to lower temperatures, as the gas is overionised for its temperature.

The background thus lowers the cooling below $10^6$ K for $U \gtrsim 10^{-1}$ and below $10^5$ K for $U \gtrsim 10^{-3}$, more so for harder spectra, and abundances varying by factors of a few between elements change the rates by similar factors ([Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) 2009, §6). Simulations of galaxy formation usually ignore metal lines or assume CIE with all metals scaled together.
